Scaling Decision Analysis with Hyperbolic Trade Space Identification

IN THIS ARTICLE
Appendix A. Register of prior art¶
A.1 The register¶
Decision problems, trade studies and multi-criterion optimization

Trade studies in systems engineering practice

What it does. Describes the trade study as a documented decision activity, with elicited criteria weights and known susceptibility to human bias. It surveys the published state of trade-off analytics.
What this work shares with it or takes from it. The setting, a decision for which an answer and a justification are required.
What this work does differently, extends or adds. This work fits its weights from the table and reruns the analysis when the data change. The study is a computation with a recorded environment, not a document.
Cites. Hirshorn et al. 2017; Baker and Whalen 2012; Chilli et al. 2026; Smith et al. 2007; Parnell et al. 2021

Decision analysis and multiple-criteria methods

What it does. Decision analysis with a normative versus descriptive distinction. Multiple-objective preferences and value trade-offs. Weighted-criteria comparison methods (Pugh, the analytic hierarchy process, Kepner-Tregoe), with weights elicited. A decision-management framework based on decision theory and cost analysis.
What this work shares with it or takes from it. The weights are trade-off rates.
What this work does differently, extends or adds. This work fits its weights from the data's edge and elicits none.
Cites. Howard 1988; Keeney and Raiffa 1993; Baker and Whalen 2012; Parnell 2016

Model-based systems engineering

What it does. MBSE centred on the system model as the sole source of truth; a systematic review of the evidence for MBSE benefits, opening from document-based practice.
What this work shares with it or takes from it. The trade space as a model rather than a document.
What this work does differently, extends or adds. This work is a model-based trade analysis, not an MBSE methodology; the system model stays behind its abstraction from configuration to attributes.
Cites. Madni and Sievers 2018; Henderson and Salado 2021

Multidisciplinary design analysis and optimization (MDAO)

What it does. Couples the disciplinary analyses of a design through shared coupling variables and solves the coupled system inside an optimizer: a survey of the field by its conceptual components, a classification of its architectures by problem formulation and decomposition strategy, a diagram standard for them (which extends the design structure matrix with process flow), a framework that solves the coupled models and computes their coupled derivatives, and a textbook treatment. Each run returns an optimum of a stated objective under constraints.
What this work shares with it or takes from it. The setting of the case, a design loop in which a configuration is evaluated by coupled disciplinary models into attributes and the design variables are chosen against several objectives. The case's coupling table is a design structure matrix.
What this work does differently, extends or adds. This work fits the table the evaluations leave behind and reports the trade-offs they exercised. It gives one exponent vector per monomial term and a level for every design, whatever produced the table. It does not search, couple disciplines or need derivatives; an MDAO run is one way to fill the table.
Cites. Sobieszczanski-Sobieski and Haftka 1997; Martins and Lambe 2013; Lambe and Martins 2012; Gray et al. 2019; Martins and Ning 2021

Tradespace exploration

What it does. Value-focused broad exploration of design alternatives, a design-by-shopping interface in which the decision-maker forms a preference by viewing many good designs, user-guided samplers and linked visualization with the Pareto frontier displayed, and tradespace exploration for resilient systems.
What this work shares with it or takes from it. The setting, a table of evaluated designs.
What this work does differently, extends or adds. This work fits and reports a coordinate system and a score.
Why this is not just the neighbour. This is not just tradespace exploration: exploration samples and shows the designs, while this fits the table and returns the trade-offs and a score for each design in coordinates that can be inverted.
Cites. Ross et al. 2004; Ross and Hastings 2005; Ross et al. 2010; Stump et al. 2003; Simpson et al. 2008; Stump et al. 2009; Spero et al. 2014

Pareto and multiobjective optimization

What it does. Returns a nondominated set of designs, and finds Pareto-optimal solutions by scalarization, for example the weighting method; evolutionary algorithms generate nondominated sets. A nondominated set says which designs survive, by a hard comparison.
What this work shares with it or takes from it. A maximizer of a weighted geometric mean with positive exponents is Pareto-efficient (lemma L11), and the weights act as a trade-off.
What this work does differently, extends or adds. This work returns a coordinate system and exponents: the fit says what trades against what, with one exponent vector per term, a band of near-boundary designs and an effective sample size. It compares by a soft maximum, not by dominance, and identifies a surface from a table without generating designs.
Why this is not just the neighbour. This is not just a Pareto front: a front is the set of designs that no other design dominates, while the fit returns a reference surface with coordinates, and for each term the exponents that give the elasticities between metrics.
Cites. Miettinen 1999; Ehrgott 2005; Zitzler and Thiele 1999; Deb et al. 2002
Machine learning, dimensionality reduction and kernel methods

Principal component analysis (PCA)

What it does. Finds uncorrelated components that successively maximize variance, reducing dimensionality while keeping interpretability.
What this work shares with it or takes from it. The question "what explains this table", answered by an unsupervised summary in a few directions.
What this work does differently, extends or adds. This work's criterion is a one-sided soft maximum, not variance, and its exponents live on the simplex, so its components are trade-offs between metrics. The two chain. This work belongs beside PCA in unsupervised learning and feature engineering and is not a PCA.
Why this is not just the neighbour. This is not just PCA: PCA explains variance, which is analysis and says nothing about what trades against what, while the fit explains the edge of the table by the trade-offs among metrics.
Cites. Jolliffe and Cadima 2016

Compositional and log-ratio PCA

What it does. PCA of compositional data, and isometric logratio transformations for compositional data analysis.
What this work shares with it or takes from it. The chart Z = Bu is in the spirit of an isometric logratio chart.
What this work does differently, extends or adds. This work's criterion is one-sided; its weights lie on the simplex, and its chart is paired with the level log V and is reversible to the metrics and to attribute units.
Why this is not just the neighbour. This is not just log-ratio analysis: the log-ratio chart is one half of the coordinate system, and the other half is the level log V relative to a fitted one-sided surface, which log-ratio analysis does not fit.
Cites. Aitchison 1983; Aitchison 1986; Egozcue et al. 2003

Kernel PCA

What it does. PCA in an implicit feature space, reached through kernel functions: principal components of data in a feature space nonlinearly related to the input space.
What this work shares with it or takes from it. Kernel PCA is the feature-engineering kin of the kernel direction that stays compatible with geometric programming.
What this work does differently, extends or adds. This work builds no kernel and maximizes no variance: it fits a one-sided posynomial edge, and its kernel direction is documented, not built.
Cites. Schölkopf et al. 1998

HoroPCA (PCA in hyperbolic space)

What it does. PCA components carried into hyperbolic space with horospherical projections (a parameterization of subspaces, a projection and a variance objective), for data that lives on a hyperbolic manifold such as an embedded tree; it maximizes a nonconvex variance over frames by gradient ascent with restarts, and gives no certificate.
What this work shares with it or takes from it. The closest existing analog in setting: unsupervised, a table in and coordinates out, with the number of components chosen by the analyst, and PCA as a step that can follow.
What this work does differently, extends or adds. This work estimates a one-sided edge, not a variance over frames, by convex programs on a simplex with a certificate, and it uses no negative-curvature geometry; on the synthetic tables of Appendix C, the leading components of PCA and HoroPCA recover the exponents only where the generator made them the direction of largest spread.
Cites. Chami et al. 2021

Data envelopment analysis (DEA)

What it does. Evaluates the relative efficiency of each decision-making unit by mathematical programming on the observed data, with weights obtained from the data. It has variants for scale and returns to scale, a super-efficiency variant that ranks efficient units against a reference technology spanned by all other units, and Malmquist productivity indices across data sets.
What this work shares with it or takes from it. A one-sided envelope of the data, a score per unit, scores beyond the boundary (V above 1) and a boundary that moves between data sets.
What this work does differently, extends or adds. This work fits global exponents per term, not per-unit weights, and reports a band, not extreme units. Its exponent vector per term is a global named structure, and its surface is piecewise log-linear, with constant elasticities within a term. Its metrics are larger-is-better with homogeneity by the simplex in place of inputs, outputs and a returns-to-scale choice. Its boundary is a soft maximum with a temperature, a band and an effective sample size in place of a deterministic boundary set by extreme units. It solves one convex program and K refits, and returns coordinates and a pull-back to attribute units in place of a score and a peer set.
Why this is not just the neighbour. This is not just DEA: DEA scores each unit against the envelope under weights obtained from the data, while the fit learns one set of exponents per term from the whole table and reports where a design sits relative to its term and which metrics trade there.
Cites. Charnes et al. 1978; Banker et al. 1984; Andersen and Petersen 1993; Färe et al. 1994; Allen et al. 1997; Dyson et al. 2001

Multiplicative DEA

What it does. A multiplicative efficiency model whose envelopment is piecewise Cobb-Douglas (Charnes, Cooper, Seiford and Stutz, read by title). Linear programs for piecewise log-linear estimation of the efficient boundary of production, derived from properties postulated for the production possibility set, which admit increasing marginal products and S-shaped production functions and estimate technical inefficiencies, rates of substitution and transformation, marginal products and returns to scale (Banker and Maindiratta, read by abstract).
What this work shares with it or takes from it. A piecewise log-linear fit to the data from above, each piece a product of powers; a score per unit against it.
What this work does differently, extends or adds. This work fits one exponent vector per term to every design at once under a soft maximum with a temperature and a band, the number of terms the analyst's choice. Its metrics are larger-is-better with homogeneity by the simplex in place of inputs and outputs, and it returns coordinates and a pull-back to attribute units in place of a score per unit.
Cites. Charnes et al. 1982; Charnes et al. 1983; Banker and Maindiratta 1986

The hyperbolic graph measure of efficiency

What it does. Efficiency measures on the graph of a technology, inputs and outputs both freely variable, in place of measures that hold the outputs or the inputs fixed (the chapter "Hyperbolic Graph Efficiency Measures", read by abstract).
What this work shares with it or takes from it. A boundary of a technology, and each unit measured against it with no quantity held fixed: V scores a design against the fitted reference surface with every metric free.
What this work does differently, extends or adds. This work fits products of powers of larger-is-better metrics to a table under a soft maximum and scales no inputs against outputs. Its word "hyperbolic" is defined in Appendix D, and is not this measure.
Cites. Färe et al. 1985

Production and distance functions

What it does. The Cobb-Douglas production form, and the Shephard distance function with its duality.
What this work shares with it or takes from it. The weighted geometric mean with exponents summing to one is the constant-returns Cobb-Douglas form; V read as a scaling factor to the boundary is a distance-function reading.
What this work does differently, extends or adds. This work takes attributes in place of inputs, and fits the form from a table of designs with a soft maximum.
Why this is not just the neighbour. This is not just a production or distance function: those are economic forms for inputs and outputs, while here the same product of powers is fitted to a table of attributes, as the one-sided boundary of conditioned metrics.
Cites. Cobb and Douglas 1928; Shephard 1970; Färe and Primont 1995; Farrell 1957

Deterministic and stochastic frontiers

What it does. Estimates frontier production functions by programming, corrected ordinary least squares or maximum likelihood, and stochastic frontiers with one-sided error.
What this work shares with it or takes from it. A one-sided criterion on log-linear residuals.
What this work does differently, extends or adds. This work uses a smooth soft maximum over a simplex-constrained geometric mean in place of a shifted intercept, and K terms.
Why this is not just the neighbour. This is not just a stochastic frontier: a frontier estimate shifts the intercept of a log-linear fit to bound the data, with a one-sided error, while the fit here is a soft maximum over a simplex-constrained geometric mean, with a temperature, a band and K terms.
Cites. Aigner and Chu 1968; Afriat 1972; Richmond 1974; Greene 1980; Aigner et al. 1977; Battese 1992
Solvers, convex optimization and generalized geometric programs

Geometric programming and max-affine fitting

What it does. Geometric programming with generalized posynomials, aircraft design as geometric programs with fits compatible with them, softmax-affine fits compatible with geometric programming, convex piecewise-linear fits, and reweighted ℓ₁ sparsity. The sources fit regression surfaces.
What this work shares with it or takes from it. The K > 1 refit scheme and the sparsity option (reweighted ℓ₁, Candès et al. 2008), and the model class.
What this work does differently, extends or adds. This work fits an envelope, the one-sided boundary of the data, and reads its exponents as elasticities.
Why this is not just the neighbour. This is not just geometric-programming or max-affine fitting: those fit a surface through the middle of the data by regression, while this fits the edge of the data and reports the terms as trade-offs.
Cites. Boyd et al. 2007; Hoburg and Abbeel 2014; Hoburg et al. 2016; Magnani and Boyd 2009; Candès et al. 2008

Entropic risk

What it does. The entropic risk measure and its limits (the mean and the essential supremum), and entropic value-at-risk.
What this work shares with it or takes from it. The log-mean-exp soft maximum is the sample version of the entropic risk functional.
What this work does differently, extends or adds. This work uses nothing from the risk-measure side; the entries are cited to avoid confusion of the soft maximum with a risk measure.
Cites. Föllmer n.d.; Ahmadi-Javid 2012

The reading pass, one row per sentence of the paper that rests on a cited work, with the source and the place in it that was read, is paper/supplement/reading-pass.md.

Appendix B. Extended results: what the method found in the table¶

The K = 1 fit at each temperature of the sweep and the K = 1 fit within each wheel class, both regimes, are paper/supplement/temperature-sweep.md and paper/supplement/refits-by-category.md.

Of the thirteen traded metrics, two, the power margin and the battery peak power ratio, enter no contending set (the support of a term's exact-maximum fit) at K = 1 to 3 in either regime: each moves with a metric that does (|Spearman| at least 0.97, below the 0.99 at which Appendix F.5 names a pair one axis). At K = 1 in the primary regime five carry no exponent at the fit temperature; three of them (control effort, energy balance and peak slew power) enter a contending set at K = 2 or 3 or in the other regime. A zero exponent says the metric does not set the surface in this sample; a near-collinear pair is read summed.

B.2 The regimes side by side¶

Theorem 3 gives convexity, not strict convexity. For two metrics with identical columns the loss is flat along their swap, so which of a pair that nearly coincides carries the exponent is not read as a finding, and the regimes’ contending sets are compared with such a pair counted as one.

Datasets cubesat-primary, cubesat-stock:

quantity	primary	baseline
tau, frac_above_, n_eff_	41.7, 0.098, 73.6	45.7, 0.106, 47.9
band 1/tau, near-frontier designs	0.0240, 238	0.0219, 293
n_lemma	7	8
contending set	unit cost, available power, pointing error, fleet cost, momentum headroom, settling time, depth of discharge	momentum headroom, available power, total mass, settling time, pointing error, depth of discharge, unit cost, fleet cost
settling time, total mass	-0.46 (-0.46)	-0.16 (-0.14)
pointing error, control effort	-0.52 (-0.66)	-0.69 (-0.77)
settling time, pointing error	+0.36 (+0.47)	+0.68 (+0.76)

Pairs at |Spearman| 0.99 or above, read as one axis with their two exponents summed per term: primary regime: energy balance and available power; baseline regime: energy balance and available power.

The RobustScaler variant changes the K = 1 contending set: 8 metrics, 6 of the baseline's 7 among them, the largest exponent on total mass. The exponents' stability under resampling of the rows is not assessed at K = 1: the movement of an exponent vector is read from K = 2 (Appendix B.10).

B.4 The solver’s termination records¶

What each status asserts. certified: the assignment is fixed, residuals and gaps are within tolerance, no cell is empty and the margin is positive; levels 1 to 3 hold. stationary_without_margin: the same with a zero margin or an empty cell; levels 1 and 2 only. uncertified, guard_stop and iteration_cap: level 1 only, reported apart from certified. not_applicable: a penalty, the tangent step or a single term; the gaps are diagnostics. The record also holds the largest term gap, the Corollary bound, the margin, the stable radius and the shares. Each regime’s table gives the fits at K = 1, 2 and 3, each K at its own fit temperature, with the conditioning of the exponents, conditioning_: the smallest eigenvalue of the restricted Hessian at each term’s fit; a small value flags a nearly flat direction. It is read only and takes no part in a status; docs/user-guide/termination.md says how to read it.

Dataset cubesat-primary:

K	tau	band 1/tau	loss_	frac_above_	max gap	status	gamma	radius	bound	conditioning_
1	41.72	0.024	0.1218	0.09794	6.96e-06	not_applicable	nan	nan	6.96e-06	[0.17]
2	113.2	0.0088	0.08743	0.09513	below 1e-12	certified	0.0003768	2.552e-05	below 1e-12	[0.62, 0.16]

Planted terms	Largest exponent deviation after matching terms
3	0.007605

How small is a small margin. The margin gamma is in score units, the scale of the band 1/tau. Below the band, a change as small as the band can move a design to another cell. Compare it with the median over the designs of the gap between best and second-best term score. Far below it, a few designs sit on a cell boundary and most do not. The stable radius is gamma divided by twice the largest norm of a row of the log-metrics with a final entry of minus one. It is smaller than the margin and is sufficient, not tight.

B.6 Propose, predict, check¶

A proposal is the pooled configuration predicted nearest white space (where no sampled design lies) in the four-metric chart, within a radius that a stated rule sets from the data: the 2nd percentile of the pool’s nearest predicted distances to the 300 most isolated points of white space. Turning a predicted level into a bounded outcome is the analyst’s task and can fail while the fit is certified: a prediction outside the quantity’s admissible range is reported as invalid, never clipped.

Dataset cubesat-primary:

proposal	predicted log V	measured log V	difference	|diff| / band	log V within band	||dZ||	||dZ|| / band	Z within band
proposal_1	-0.03629	0.00615	0.04244	0.4537	True	0.0170	0.18	True
proposal_2	0.1031	0.1641	0.06108	0.653	True	0.07704	0.8236	True
proposal_3	-0.03629	0.00615 to 0.00616	0.042	0.454	True	0.017	0.18	True

What propose, predict, check showed. Two of the three proposals are one configuration. It showed that the regressor's predicted energy differential and predicted position of a proposal each lie within its band of the measured one (3 of 3 and 3 of 3): the error of the prediction. Where the proposals landed is a second quantity, the measured log V against zero. They reached the four-metric fitted reference surface they were aimed at by spending the battery. Their measured log V is within 0.164 of V = 1, against the band 0.094. Their batteries are 22 Wh, at the bottom of the 20 to 200 Wh range, and all 3 fail the model's depth-of-discharge feasibility condition at 0.39. The proposer's chart is the four-metric view, which has no depth-of-discharge axis. A proposer that also predicts the feasibility flags, and proposes only designs predicted to meet them, is future work. Three proposals of two designs illustrate and do not estimate a rate. A predicted crossing of V = 1 remains a hypothesis.

B.7 Stability of the conclusions under rounding-level jitter¶

The control effort and the saturated fraction are sums over the accepted steps of an adaptive integration, and the settling time is detected on its dense solution. A relative change of 1e-12 in a design variable therefore moves them (gap G-17). Datasets cubesat-primary and cubesat-stock and the six check designs were regenerated 5 times with Kp, Kd, the panel span and the battery capacity of every design multiplied by 1 + e, e uniform in [-1e-12, 1e-12]. Every quantity this paper reports was recomputed: 817 quantities, of which 345 numbers move at all. The regenerations ran on the authors' machine and the committed data in the environment of record, so a range also holds the two platforms' difference. A quantity that moves is printed to the digits that held in all 6 datasets (the committed one and the 5 regenerated). Where not even two digits held it is printed as a range, and where it is a numerical residue as a bound.

Conclusion	Quantities compared	Across the committed data and 5 regenerations
the contending sets (K = 1 and each term at K = 3)	28	identical
each term's leading metrics	12	identical
the signs of the reported associations	12	identical
the fit temperatures, to the printed 0.1	6	identical
the certificate statuses	6	identical
the pass or miss of each check design	6	identical
the count of nondominated designs on all attributes	2	identical

The printed quantities that move, with the committed value, the minimum and maximum over the 6 datasets:

Quantity	Committed	Minimum	Maximum	Printed in this paper as
largest term gap, K = 2, primary	below 1e-12	below 1e-12	below 1e-12	below 1e-12
largest term gap, K = 3, primary	below 1e-12	below 1e-12	below 1e-12	below 1e-12

B.9 Does the fit recover a planted answer: twenty tables¶

The generator of Appendix F plants three terms of known exponents. Here it draws twenty tables of 400 rows and 4 metrics, each fitted at the exact maximum and at a working temperature. Each failure is listed with the frontier rows of its thinnest planted term.

Dataset planted-3-term, the generator of Appendix F at 400 rows, 4 metrics and 3 terms, seeds 0 to 19, the solver's defaults (five restarts, random_state = 0). A table is recovered when the largest error of the exponents, after matching fitted to planted terms, is under 0.05 and at least 90% of its frontier rows lie on the right term.

Temperature	Planted tables	Recovered	Seeds not recovered
infinity	20	16 of 20	1, 3, 8, 16
50	20	16 of 20	1, 3, 8, 16

The tables not recovered, with the frontier rows of their thinnest planted term (a term needs at least 5 frontier rows, one more than there are metrics, to be determined by them), whether each was recovered at the exact maximum and at temperature 50, and at temperature 50 the solver's status, the largest exponent error and the share of frontier rows on the right term:

Seed	Frontier rows, thinnest term	Recovered at the exact maximum	Recovered at temperature 50	Status at 50	Largest error at 50	Frontier rows on the right term at 50
1	5	no	no	certified	0.32	0.54
3	6	no	no	certified	0.11	0.94
8	11	no	no	certified	0.20	0.68
16	5	no	no	certified	0.34	0.64

At the exact maximum the fit of each of these tables stops without a margin (stationary_without_margin) or at the iteration cap, depending on the platform, and its errors move with it, so neither is printed: a frontier row on two term planes ties at the exact maximum and the platform's argmax and linear-program vertex choice resolve the tie (gap G-10). Whichever status a fit stopped with, its largest term gap at termination was below tol (1e-10) in 40 of the 40 fits (level 2, numerical stationarity, L12), so a stop at the iteration cap is the labels not settling and not a term left unsolved; none of the fits stopped at the cap with a gap at or above tol. What does not depend on the platform is the count and the seeds: 16 of 20 tables were recovered, and the seeds above failed, at both temperatures on macOS arm64 and on Linux amd64. Every table recovered has at least 18 frontier rows on each planted term. At temperature 50 the status and the errors, printed to two digits, were the same on both platforms.

B.10 Choosing K for the case¶

K is read from three checks of the package’s choosing-K guide, run on each dataset: the mean slack against K, read through its drop with each added term (it falls with every added term by construction); the share of the near-frontier designs held by the thinnest term; and the movement of an exponent vector under resampling. The movement is the largest, over three resamples of the rows, of the summed absolute difference to the nearest term of the refit, at most 2. The mean slack, the first share and the movement are read at the K = 1 temperature, where mean_slack_ is comparable across K and loss_ is not. The second share is read at each K’s own fit temperature. No check decides K alone. The K is the one where the largest drop in the mean slack lands, read against the other two.

Dataset cubesat-primary:

K	mean_slack_ at the K = 1 temperature, 41.7	Drop from the K before	Thinnest term's share, same temperature	Thinnest term's share, K's own	Largest movement under resampling
1	0.122	-	1.00	1.00	-
2	0.043	0.078	0.28	0.26	0.22
3	0.020	0.023	0.21	0.06	0.15
4	0.008	0.012	0.17	0.11	0.75

The checks point to K = 2.

Dataset cubesat-stock:

K	mean_slack_ at the K = 1 temperature, 45.7	Drop from the K before	Thinnest term's share, same temperature	Thinnest term's share, K's own	Largest movement under resampling
1	0.115	-	1.00	1.00	-
2	0.060	0.055	0.35	0.25	0.08
3	0.030	0.031	0.13	0.06	0.30
4	0.017	0.012	0.09	0.05	0.69

The checks point to K = 2.

Each K selects its own fit temperature by the same rule: tau = 42 and 113 at K = 1 and 2, with bands 1/tau of 0.0240 and 0.0088. The figures and the association reading use the K = 1 band at every K (gap G-9, Appendix G).

B.11 Chaining with PCA: the principal directions of the near-frontier chart¶

The trade-off report runs a principal component analysis on the chart coordinates Z of the near-frontier designs in a term’s cell. Its directions say along which combinations of metrics the near-frontier designs vary the most, with loadings on the metrics through the chart basis. The fit says which metrics bind, the analysis says where the sampled designs spread along the fitted surface. They answer different questions and the second is computed from the first’s coordinates. The sign of a direction is arbitrary; each is shown with its largest loading positive.

Dataset cubesat-primary, K = 1, designs with |log V| under the band 1/tau:

Direction	Share of the spread of the chart coordinates	Leading loadings on the metrics
1	0.361	unit cost +0.42, total mass +0.42, peak slew power +0.36
2	0.208	momentum headroom +0.58, depth of discharge +0.41, battery peak power ratio +0.31
3	0.179	battery peak power ratio +0.56, momentum headroom -0.54, depth of discharge +0.48
B.12 The requirements read after the fit¶

The fit of record is the fit on the feasible designs, with no requirement imposed before it. Six of the model’s thirteen requirements are read after the fit. The four thresholds are peak slew power 2.6 W, pointing error 0.008495 degree, control effort 0.05922 N·m·s and the launch budget, read as a limit of 15.11 kg on the total mass at 7,000 USD per kilogram, and the two cost limits on the covered cost are 350,000 USD per satellite, read at the design’s own fleet size, and 6,000,000 USD per fleet. The four limits are illustrative values chosen from a ladder of candidates by the share of the feasible designs each admits, not a mission’s; the SysML v2 model’s part budgets records each with its row of the ladder.

A requirement’s price is the log V of meeting it for the median excluded near-frontier design at the fitted K = 1 frontier: the exponent times the needed tightening in the conditioned metric, also in band widths. For the two costs and the total mass the excess column converts the price to currency, the median amount by which the excluded near-frontier designs exceed the limit. A requirement on a metric at zero exponent has no price at K = 1. The price is a feature of the fitted model on this table, not a causal exchange.

Dataset cubesat-primary:

Three design sets are fitted per dataset: every design, the feasible designs (the fit of record) and the feasible designs that also meet the four performance thresholds. The two cost limits are not among them. They are budget limits read after the fit, and no design of fleet size one passes the unit cost limit, so a set constrained by it would be a fleet-size choice, not a design-space one. Every set is conditioned by the feasible set’s conditioning, so a difference between the fits is the rows’, not a re-scaling’s, and the temperature is selected on each set’s rows by the same rule. The zero set holds the metrics at zero exponent at every temperature of the sweep.

B.13 The noise-ridge corollary against injected noise¶

Noise on the metrics shifts the exponents by a ridge (Corollary 1, L20). At half a percent in the primary regime the predicted largest shift agrees with the measured mean within the spread of the draws; in every other row the prediction exceeds the measurement, by the factors given below the table, and the contending set changes most at 10 percent noise in both regimes. The conditioning is refitted on each noisy table, which L18 does not cover, and eight draws are few.

Datasets cubesat-primary and cubesat-stock: the data of record carry no noise of their own. In its script, each raw attribute is multiplied by exp(delta z), z standard normal. The table is conditioned again and refitted at the clean fit's temperature, and the measured columns are over eight draws. The predicted shift is the corollary's formula on the support of the clean fit (its exponents above 0.001), where the corollary holds while the zero exponents stay zero. The last column, the overlap of the contending set with the clean fit's, shows where noise changes it. The noise of each metric is delta divided by the log spread of its attribute. What the table shows belongs to the fit and the conditioning together.

regime	conditioning_	delta (%)	predicted largest shift	measured largest shift (mean)	spread of the draws	contending-set Jaccard
cubesat-primary	0.167	0.5	0.005	0.006	0.016	0.98
cubesat-primary	0.167	2.0	0.079	0.008	0.013	1.00
cubesat-primary	0.167	5.0	0.492	0.081	0.025	0.83
cubesat-primary	0.167	10.0	1.968	0.152	0.038	0.76
cubesat-stock	0.207	0.5	0.075	0.046	0.022	1.00
cubesat-stock	0.207	2.0	1.196	0.107	0.023	0.97
cubesat-stock	0.207	5.0	7.473	0.136	0.032	0.87
cubesat-stock	0.207	10.0	29.890	0.167	0.036	0.77

Where the prediction exceeds the measured mean by more than the spread of the draws (7 of 8 rows), it does so by a factor of 1.6 to 179 (predicted over measured).

B.14 The decision: the shortlist and the rules¶

The shortlist is the feasible designs that pass all six requirements (B.12) and lie within the near-frontier band of both the K = 1 and the K = 2 fit (the K = 1 band for both, as at every K). Four rules pick from it, one stakeholder per objective and one between two of them:

sponsor: least fleet cost;

manufacturer: least unit cost;

operator: largest coverage probability;

between sponsor and operator: the design whose larger shortfall on fleet cost and coverage probability is smallest, each scaled from its best to its worst value on the shortlist (minimax regret).

The coverage probability is the SysML v2 model’s CoverageProbability, read from the fleet size and the pointing error. Each named design is asserted to lie in the shortlist and to pass the six requirements.

The shortlist ordered by fleet cost. The last column marks the designs that no other design on the shortlist beats on both fleet cost and coverage probability. The level is log V at each K, positive beyond the fitted surface.

Dataset cubesat-primary. Pointing error is printed to six decimals, so that a near tie reads as it is: designs 999 and 190 agree to five decimals; designs 1282 and 530 agree to five decimals.

Dataset cubesat-stock: the baseline regime (stock gains) has 2,355 feasible designs, 523 that pass the six requirements and 19 within the band of both fits.

Appendix C. HoroPCA beside this work: a tested hypothesis¶
C.1 The hypothesis and what would refute it¶

HoroPCA Chami et al., 2021 carries principal component analysis (PCA), the directions along which a table spreads most, into hyperbolic space. This work finds the edge of the table instead: a vector of exponents defining a surface that bounds the rows from above. The hypothesis, committed with its refutation conditions before any table of this experiment existed (record), is that PCA and HoroPCA follow the spread and this work follows the edge. Tables of kind A keep the edge and vary the spread ratio ρ, the standard deviation along the edge’s vector (the planted exponents) over that along the largest direction orthogonal to it. Kind B moves the vector by a fraction t from the depth direction of the package’s generator (equal exponents, where it puts its spread) to one metric, and kind C removes the edge and still scores the same vector. A method recovers the vector when the absolute cosine (|cos|, agreement of directions ignoring sign) of its direction with it reaches the threshold, 0.9.

H1 (A, B): this work recovers the vector in 6/6 tables; refuted by any cell under 6/6.
H2 (A): PCA and HoroPCA (R = 0.25, R = 1) recover it in 6/6 at ρ ≥ 2 and in 0/6 at ρ ≤ 0.5; refuted by a cell outside either bound.
H3 (B, t = 0.9): PCA recovers it in at most 1/6 and this work in 6/6; refuted by PCA above 1/6.
H4 (C): this work recovers it in at most 1/6, and PCA at ρ = 2 in 6/6; refuted by either failing.
C.2 The experiment¶

Four metrics, 300 rows, six tables per setting. In A the vector is random and ρ runs from 0.25 to 4. In B, t runs from 0 to 0.9. In C, ρ is 2 or 0.5 and the rows spread symmetrically along the vector. Each table goes to this work (one term), to PCA (first component) and to HoroPCA (first direction; the authors’ code, five restarts). HoroPCA needs a ball (the disk that models hyperbolic space), so the log-metrics are centred and scaled until the farthest lies at distance R from the centre: nearly flat at R = 0.25, moderately curved at R = 1.

C.3 Results¶

Chance reaches |cos| ≥ 0.9 with probability 0.037 for a random direction and 0.152 for a random point of the simplex, where this work's exponents live.

Table 1:Appendix C’s hypothesis table: tables out of six in which each method recovered the vector. H1 to H4 are listed in C.1; HoroPCA cells read R = 0.25, R = 1; no prediction at ρ = 1. Source: results.json, readout.md.

Verdict	Table	This work	PCA	HoroPCA, R = 0.25 and 1
survives (H1, H2)	A, ρ = 0.25	6/6	0/6	0/6, 0/6
survives (H1, H2)	A, ρ = 0.5	6/6	0/6	0/6, 0/6
survives (H1)	A, ρ = 1	6/6	1/6	0/6, 0/6
survives (H1, H2)	A, ρ = 2	6/6	6/6	6/6, 6/6
survives (H1, H2)	A, ρ = 4	6/6	6/6	6/6, 6/6
survives (H1)	B, t = 0	6/6	6/6	6/6, 6/6
survives (H1)	B, t = 0.3	6/6	0/6	0/6, 0/6
survives (H1)	B, t = 0.6	6/6	0/6	0/6, 0/6
survives (H1, H3)	B, t = 0.9	6/6	0/6	0/6, 0/6
survives (H4)	C, ρ = 0.5	0/6	0/6	0/6, 0/6
survives (H4)	C, ρ = 2	0/6	6/6	6/6, 6/6
survives	All parts			

Every check in the 11 cells holds, so every part survives.

At ρ = 2, removing the edge takes this work from 6 of 6 tables to 0 of 6 and leaves PCA at 6 of 6.

C.4 What the table shows¶

In these controlled constructions this work tracks the planted upper boundary, while the leading directions of PCA and HoroPCA track the variation in the sampled designs, so their agreement depends on how the boundary’s orientation relates to the sample’s spread, and the methods answer different questions. Appendix B.11 chains them, running PCA on the chart of the near-frontier designs.

PCA's component of least variance (its fourth) recovers the vector in 5 of 6 tables at ρ = 0.25 and at t = 0.9, and in none of the other nine cells. It is outside the hypothesis: variance ranking alone does not identify which component represents the boundary.

Reading limits. (a) The embedding is part of the treatment: the centre, the scale R and the exponential map at the origin into the ball are our choices, so the experiment distinguishes the implemented pipelines under it and cannot establish that HoroPCA could never recover boundary information; a second embedding was not run, and R = 3 is in the record, not tested. (b) The tables favour clean identification (noise-free, four metrics, 30 percent of the rows on the edge, a fixed temperature) and are not tree-like, the data HoroPCA was built for: a mechanism demonstration. (c) Six tables per cell establish separation, not population recovery rates; every 6/6 is a count. HoroPCA ran one direction at its default settings.

HoroPCA’s loss and solver, beside this work’s convex fit and its gap: how the formalism and the solvers differ.

Appendix E. Proofs, step for step with their scripts¶

What the scripts establish. The lemma scripts are a substitute for pencil-and-paper derivation, not formal verification. They sit between a free-form proof, which no tool checks, and a proof assistant, whose language and friction put the derivation beyond most readers. Each step is one line of symbolic algebra that the reader can run against its sentence, on the stack the paper already uses. A step checks the algebra it states under the ledger’s assumptions; it does not check that those assumptions hold for a table of designs (Appendix G). The formal prover in this work is the Z3 solver behind the requirement checks of the case’s system model (the repository tier of Appendix F), and formal verification is said only of those checks.

Each proof is one file of derivation/, and each step of the proof is one line of that file. The address of a step is its file and its tag (L2.1, L2.2, ...), and the tag is unique within the file. The record holds 24 results in 22 files and 549 steps. Every step is a line of code. It is one of three kinds:

computed (368 steps): SymPy derives the step's condition and the line asserts it.
by a declared axiom (169 steps): the line applies an entry of the axiom ledger that the script imported by name; the entry's wording and citation are shown beside the step, and the link gives the import line, the declaration's address in the proof's own file.
by a proved result (12 steps): the line applies the result of an earlier proof that the script imported; the step names that proof's statement and links to its entry.

A step that SymPy cannot derive is an explicit hypothesis or an entry of the ledger. Two results may share a file. L14 proves Proposition 3 and its corollary, the approximate certificate; each names the same file and owns its own steps.

Result	File	Steps	Computed	By axiom	By proved result
L1. Homogeneity and invariant form: V(k m) = k V(m), V(m / V) = 1	lemma_01_homogeneity.py	8	4	4	0
L2. Uniform shift: J_tau(w; U + c 1^T) = J_tau(w; U) + c	lemma_02_shift.py	8	4	4	0

L3. gradient of the soft-maximum loss and the full simplex KKT conditions

File. lemma_03_gradient_kkt.py. Statement. d/dt J_tau(w + t v)|_0 = sum_i p_i (v . u_i) = sum_j v_j (sum_i p_i u_ij), p = softmax(tau U w), so grad J_tau = g = U^T p. At a minimizer w of J_tau on the simplex, with lambda = w.g: g_j >= lambda for all j and w_j (g_j - lambda) = 0 (complementary slackness); the equal-gradient condition (U^T p)_j = lambda is the case w_j > 0, so all components are equal at an interior minimizer. Balanced hard-support geometry (tau = infinity) does not imply an interior finite-tau minimizer: a constructed instance has a balanced hard optimum and a finite-tau minimizer on the boundary with g_j > lambda at the inactive j

Hypotheses. A2, A3 and I10 (the first-order conditions) and, for the facet-normal reading of R1 (an earlier result), $w_j>0$ for every $j$ at the fit temperature, taken on its own (Appendix E, L3.9) .

L11. A V-maximizer is Pareto-efficient (weighted-sum scalarization; converse not claimed)

File. lemma_11_pareto.py. Statement. For w in the simplex and u' = u + delta, delta >= 0: w.u' - w.u = sum_j w_j delta_j >= 0, strictly > 0 whenever some j has w_j > 0 and delta_j > 0; hence V(u') >= V(u) with strict inequality under the same condition, and a maximizer of V = exp(w.u - beta) over a set S is not dominated by any member of S in the contending metrics (w_j > 0): weakly efficient in all d metrics, efficient when every w_j > 0. At K > 1, kappa = infinity, a maximizer of Phi = max_k (theta_k.u - beta_k) is not dominated through any term active at it (theta_k0.delta = 0), so it is weakly efficient in all d metrics. The converse is not claimed: only the log-convex part of a front is reached by some w

Hypotheses (premises the script declares): u' = u + delta with delta_j >= 0 for every j (carried by the symbols dl (nonnegative)); some j has w_j > 0 and delta_j > 0 (carried by the symbols wp (positive), dp (positive)); delta_j >= eps > 0 for all j (carried by the symbols eps (positive), gam (nonnegative))

E.2 The several-term fit, its levels of guarantee and the tension (L12 to L22)¶

Restarts, stopping and the polish. For each restart the package starts from random centres in the chart and alternates the labels and the refits of Section 5; a restart stops when the labels are fixed or the iteration limit is reached, and by the descent rule (L15): if the mean slack rose, stop with the previous state. The returned fit is the restart whose mean slack is smallest. At finite 
𝜏
τ each term’s refit (SLSQP) is followed by five reduced Newton steps (the polish) that close the term gap SLSQP alone leaves (G-16), and the soft alternation ends with a joint polish; the status refers to the polished point, and the guarantees to the exact minimizers it approximates.

L12. term first-order gap: 0 <= f(w) - f* <= G, G = 0 iff the simplex KKT holds

File. lemma_12_term_gap.py. Statement. For the term refit f(w) = J_tau(U w) - ubar_k.w on the simplex (convex by L4), g = grad f(w) and G = g.w - min_j g_j = sum_j w_j (g_j - min g) >= 0, with G = 0 iff w_j (g_j - min g) = 0 for every j (L3's simplex KKT: g_j >= lambda, w_j (g_j - lambda) = 0, lambda = w.g); and 0 <= f(w) - f* <= G. For the fitted reference surface (finite tau, Phi = 0, V > 1 possible) this is the stopping certificate of a term; for the tau = inf supporting envelope, with the LP duals p, G is the LP duality gap (I1)

Hypotheses (premises the script declares): f(w) - f* >= 0 because f* is the minimum (carried by the symbols c_nn (nonnegative))

L13. assignment margin: score changes below gamma/2 keep the maximizing terms; stable radius

File. lemma_13_margin.py. Statement. Term scores s_ik = w_k.u_i - beta_k = theta_k.(u_i, -1), the energy differential (log V) of design i against term k; margin gamma = min_i [s_i,k(i) - max_{k != k(i)} s_ik] > 0 (R2). If every term score changes by at most delta, every row's new gap to every other term is >= gamma - 2 delta, positive when 2 delta < gamma, so each maximizing term is unchanged and unique. With Cauchy-Schwarz (I9), |Delta theta_k.(u_i, -1)| <= ||Delta theta_k||_2 ||(u_i, -1)||_2, so 2 delta < gamma holds whenever max_k ||Delta theta_k||_2 < gamma / (2 max_i ||(u_i, -1)||_2): an assignment-stable neighborhood of the fitted reference surface's terms

L14. Proposition 3

Shared script. Proposition 3 and Approximate-certificate corollary are proved by this one file, which numbers its 20 steps in one sequence; each proof lists its own steps in the file's order, so the tags of one skip the steps the other owns.

File. lemma_14_assignment_stable.py. Statement. Level 3 is for step B, unpenalized, with reaches beta_k = J_tau(U w_k) only. L16. soft-weight alternation: symmetry, the fixed-weight bound and the generalized posynomial

File. lemma_16_soft_fit_alternation.py. Statement. Soft-weight alternation: with weights alpha_ik = softmax_k(kappa score_ik) from the current model, (i) a convex program symmetric in the terms attains its minimum with all terms equal, so the loss over all terms is not convex in them in general; (ii) the soft model's slack is bounded above by the fixed-weight loss, with equality at the weights' own model; (iii) the fixed-weight loss is a generalized posynomial in exp(w).

Hypotheses. A1, A2, A3, A6, I14 (Jensen's inequality) and I15 (generalized posynomial).

L17. the soft-fit certificate: Hessian D - kappa M and the second-order test

File. lemma_17_soft_fit_certificate.py. Statement. L18. Gaussian cumulant with a noise covariance; the conditioning maps error to noise

File. lemma_18_noise_covariance.py. Statement. Under a Gaussian noise vector with mean 0 and covariance Sigma (A9): (1/tau) log E exp(tau w.eps) = tau w'Sigma w / 2; for diagonal Sigma, tau Sum_j w_j^2 sigma_j^2 / 2; for equal sigma_j it is the L6 value tau sigma^2 ||w||^2 / 2. Under log-affine conditioning with a_j = 0 and a multiplicative measurement error log rt_j = log r_j + zeta_j, zeta_j ~ N(0, delta_j^2) (A10), the conditioned noise is eps_j = alpha_j zeta_j, sigma_j = |alpha_j| delta_j = delta_j / s_j with s_j := 1/|alpha_j| the log spread; to first order delta_j is a relative error

Hypotheses. none beyond the declared axioms below.

L19. Noise shift of the soft maximum: 0 <= shift <= min(tau v^2/2, E max z)

File. lemma_19_noise_shift_bound.py. Statement. For clean scores s_i and independent N(0, v^2) noise draws z_i (A11), tau > 0, n >= 2 and LME the log-mean-exp: 0 <= E LME_tau(s + z) - LME_tau(s) <= min(tau v^2 / 2, E max_i z_i), and E max_i z_i <= v sqrt(2 log n) (from E max z <= log(n)/lam + lam v^2/2 for every lam > 0); tau v^2 / 2 and v sqrt(2 log n) cross at tau* = 2 sqrt(2 log n) / v. An upper bound that is not tight: the shift equals neither bound, and tau* is a rule of thumb, not a sharp threshold

L20. Noise on the metrics is a ridge on the exponents: the first-order shift

File. lemma_20_noise_ridge.py. Statement. With rows u_i + eps_i, eps_i Gaussian with covariance Sigma (A9), the expected noisy exponential J^R(w) = (1/tau) log E (1/n) Sum_i exp(tau w.(u_i + eps_i)) equals J_tau(w) + (tau/2) w'Sigma w. At a clean minimizer w* with support T (its positive exponents, the active face) and strict complementarity (the gradient exceeds lambda off T, so the zero exponents stay zero), with C = Cov_p(u) positive definite on the tangent space of the face, the vectors that sum to zero and vanish off T (R3), the first-order condition of J^R on the face, with the product of noise and shift set aside, is P C P delta = - P Sigma w*, so delta = - (P C P)^+ P Sigma w* (P the projector onto the tangent space of the face, P = I - 11'/d when every exponent is positive; delta vanishes off T; for Sigma = sigma^2 I, delta = - sigma^2 (P C P)^+ P w*, with tau cancelled). When the shift is not zero its norm is at most ||P Sigma w*|| / m, m the smallest eigenvalue of C on the tangent space (conditioning_ = tau m), and delta'Sigma w* <= - m ||delta||^2 <= 0: the ridge falls at first order. First order only: no bound on the remainder, and no statement that the noisy fit of a finite table lands at the minimizer of J^R

Hypotheses (premises the script declares): When the shift is not zero (carried by the symbols nd (positive)); The support T of w*: chi_j = 1 on T, 0 off it (carried by the symbols chi (nonnegative)); The support has sf = Sum_j chi_j coordinates (carried by the symbols sf (positive)); Strict complementarity: g_j - lambda > 0 off T (carried by the symbols gapf (positive))

Ledger. Axioms imported and applied: A1, A3, A5, A9, I18, I9, R3. Proved results imported: L18_RESULT (from lemma_18_noise_covariance), L3_RESULT (from lemma_03_gradient_kkt), L4_RESULT (from lemma_04_convexity). Exported: L20_RESULT. 63 steps.

L22. Small-temperature expansion of the log-mean-exp; the variance on centred metrics

File. lemma_22_small_temperature.py. Statement. For scores s_i = w.u_i over n rows and tau > 0: N(tau) = log((1/n) Sum_i exp(tau s_i)) has N(0) = 0, N'(0) = mean, N''(0) = variance, N'''(0) = third central moment, so the log-mean-exp N(tau)/tau has the Taylor polynomial mean + (tau/2) variance + (tau^2/6) mu_3 at zero. On centred log-metrics (Sum_i u_ij = 0) the mean is zero and the variance is the quadratic form w'Cw with C_jl = (1/n) Sum_i u_ij u_il, nonnegative for real scores. The neglected term and the uniformity of the remainder on the simplex are not bounded here

Hypotheses. none beyond the declared axioms below.

E.3 The checker and the record¶

The checker passes only when every proof names one file. Every step of every file belongs to exactly one proof. The prose steps and the script steps correspond one to one and in order. Every step by a declared axiom applies an entry that exists in the ledger and that the file imports. The entries a file imports, applies and lists as used are the same set. A proved result is imported from an earlier proof and applied by a step. A symbol declared positive, nonnegative or real is declared as a premise, never assumed silently: the last column of the second table counts them per file, and in L14 the premise that every exponent vector minimizes its term loss enters as the sign of the nonnegative symbol dfk. The fingerprint of each step (its tag, prose and line of code) and of each ledger entry equals the record's.

Item	Value
Record	derivation/proof_map_record.json
Record version	2
SHA-256 of the record	25ccfa1d00521f59082b1cff65bcbdd7b8529ee95371e7301435108e3542b268
Lemma files, steps	22, 549
Ledger entries fingerprinted	37
Check the scripts against the record	uv run python -m derivation.proofmap
Also run every computed step	uv run python -m derivation.proofmap --execute
Refresh the record (deliberate; prints what moved)	uv run python -m derivation.proofmap --update

F.3 Feasibility¶

Each condition is a flag the simulation returns for a design: settled (the slew settles within the horizon), momentum_ok (peak wheel momentum within the wheel class's maximum), and power_ok and dod_ok (the model's power and depth-of-discharge constraints). A design that fails a condition alone is counted in fails alone; removed in this order counts the designs it removes after the conditions above it.

Dataset cubesat-primary:

condition	fails alone	removed in this order	remaining

power_ok	0	0	2929
momentum_ok	540	473	2456
dod_ok	389	322	2134
all attributes positive	0	0	2134

F.5 The table of metrics¶

Each metric is m_j = exp((o_j log(a_j + s_j) - c_j) / k_j) for attribute a_j, with orientation o_j (+1 larger is better, -1 smaller is better), shift s_j = 0, and the offset c_j and log scale k_j of the conditioning above. The table of metrics, one row per kept design and one column per metric, is where the model ends and the method begins.

Dataset cubesat-primary: 2,134 designs by 13 metrics; the first 5 rows:

The association of every pair of traded metrics over the feasible designs is the step that would find two metrics the data cannot tell apart. A pair at |Spearman| 0.99 or above is named one axis, and its two exponents are read summed per term in B.1 and B.2. The baseline regime’s first rows and every pair are in paper/supplement/metric-associations.md.

F.8 The cost model¶

The unit cost and the fleet cost price a fleet of one to thirty identical satellites of this 12U class. Each part, and the assembly, integration and test line, has a single-unit price that depends on the configuration and a fleet pricing curve, a fixed share paid once plus a learning curve in the fleet size, so the unit cost falls and the fleet cost rises as the fleet grows; launch is 7,000 USD per kilogram of the total mass. The part prices are 2026 vendor and marketplace listings; the assembly line, 100,000 USD for one satellite, has no source, and the shares and slopes of the curves are choices, not findings. The fleet cost requirement holds the covered cost under 6,000,000 USD, inside the sponsor’s full budget of 8,000,000 USD. The remaining 2,000,000 USD is the allowance for what is not covered. The unit cost limit is 350,000 USD per satellite at the design’s own fleet size, which no single satellite of this class clears. For scale, a 1U series can sit below 40,000 USD a unit, and larger series are dearer. The covered cost is a lower bound on a full costing: it covers the bus (structure, power, radios and computer), the attitude-control parts, assembly and test, and wholesale launch, and leaves out the payload and its instruments, communications, flight software, environmental testing beyond the assembly line, licensing, a ground station network and operations, the launch slot minimum and broker fees.

What else the case leaves out, and what each omission does to a reading of it, is in examples/adcs/case-model.md, “What the case leaves out”.

I14	Jensen's inequality: if f is convex, x_1, ..., x_K are in its domain and theta_1, ..., theta_K >= 0 with sum_k theta_k = 1, then f(sum_k theta_k x_k) <= sum_k theta_k f(x_k)	Boyd & Vandenberghe, Convex Optimization, section 3.1.8 (Jensen's inequality and extensions; the two-point form is the definition of convexity, (3.1) of section 3.1.1); read in the authors' PDF https://web.stanford.edu/~boyd/cvxbook/bv_cvxbook.pdf	L16

A term with few frontier rows is reported with its exponents and counted near-frontier rows; its exponents are read as a family member, not a point.
G-8. The tangent step and the penalty outside Proposition 3	D: the near-frontier rows of a term lie on one hyperplane in log space. Penalty: small exponents are noise.	Monotone descent for D; local optimality under D or the penalty; that 
𝐺
𝑘
 of the unpenalized 
𝑓
𝑘
 says anything about the penalized fit (I8 fails for a nonconvex objective).	Under D or the penalty the fit is an option; the certificate columns are read as diagnostics and no local-optimality statement is made.

G-10. Ties on ridge rows and platform ties	Ties have probability zero for continuous data at finite

G-11. Statistical consistency under noise and refitting	For a consistency statement: i.i.d. sampling of designs, integrable log-metrics, identifiability of 
𝑤
, and a rule linking 
𝜏
 to 
𝑁

𝑤

𝑁
, noise and 
𝜏
; the noise corollary states a movement of the stated size under the stated noise, and the rates are not certified.
G-12. Identifiability of the contending set under finite, biased observations	The design box and sampling cover the boundary in every metric; a metric with a small exponent is distinguishable from one with none.	That the sampled designs are unbiased with respect to the attainable set; that a metric absent from the contending set is absent from the true trade-off; that a metric's presence in the contending set is a property of the model, since it belongs to the sample, the conditioning and the noise together, and L20's shift is large where conditioning_ is small, which is where presence is least identified. L20's formula holds on the support of a fit with a zero exponent and not with the full-simplex projector (test), nor once the noise lifts a boundary exponent off zero (..._when_the_noise_overcomes_the_complementarity_margin_neither_formula_holds).	The contending set is a statement about the sampled region; a metric's absence or presence is read together with conditioning_, the sampling regime and the injected-noise table (Appendix B, B.13).
G-13. Frontier recovery, fitted against the true Pareto boundary	The family of planted frontiers (monomial terms, 
≥
𝑑
+
1
 frontier rows, noise 0) represents the boundaries of interest; 
𝐾
 is chosen by the analyst.	Conditions on the data under which the fit recovers the boundary; that the attainable set of a real system is a posynomial frontier.	A recovery rate is quoted at its 
𝑁
, 
𝑑
, 
𝐾
, 
𝜏
 and noise. The fitted reference surface of a real system is a model of the data, and the Pareto boundary is a question for new experiments.
G-14. Global convergence (not pursued)	None sought.	Global optimality; uniqueness of the partition; that more restarts or a longer run improves the returned fit.	Every 
𝐾
>
1
 result is a local optimum of its run, reported with random_state.
G-15. Restarts and local optima	Mean slack ranks the restarts.	That the returned run's assignment is the best among local optima; that the Corollary bound is a bound across restarts; that the mean-slack selector picks the restart with the best recovery.	The certificate is read as a statement about the returned assignment. Two fits of different assignments are compared by objective_, not by their certificates.
G-16. Stationary without margin; the hypotheses of Proposition 3	The tolerances (
10
−
8
, 
10
−
9
, guard 
10
−
12
) separate noise from structure. The data lie in the margin-positive case (R2).	Positive margin on a given data set; that the radius is tight (it is sufficient, attained only for changes parallel to the design vector); that a certified fit is the best local fit (G-15).	The status states which of the three levels the fit meets: descent always, stationarity per term when the gap is small, local optimality of the fixed assignment only when certified. The stable radius is small in parameter units, so level 3 is a local statement.
G-17. The settling time as an event-detected time	The quantities are stable to rounding-level perturbation of the inputs: a relative change of 
10
−
12
 in a design variable moves the settling time by far less than a step and the step sums in their last digits. The metrics, the fits and the reading built on them move continuously with it.	That the detected time equals the last exit of the exact trajectory; that the map from design to time is continuous where the trajectory touches the band; that the printed digits of any quantity computed from the step sums are reproducible across platforms.	Read the case's printed numbers at the stated range, not at the digit. The qualitative conclusions (which metrics contend, which trade against which, the certificate status, the outcomes of the checks) are stable.
G-18. Equal mean slack does not identify the boundary	The unsampled region is where the boundary is not observed. A fit that reaches the loss floor describes the rows; which of the supporting envelopes of equal loss is the boundary is a question for data in the gap.	That the supporting envelope is the attainable boundary away from the rows; that more terms or a lower loss moves the fit toward it.	A mean slack at its floor is evidence about the rows. Do not read equal or lower loss across 
𝐾
 as evidence about the boundary between observations; place new designs in the gap to tell the envelopes apart. No change to the objective is made.

H.2 The environment of record¶

Inside the pinned linux/arm64 image the results reproduce byte for byte. Between that image and the Mac, and between it and a comparable Ubuntu image, values differ at the last digits because the platforms’ mathematical libraries differ, and the conclusions are unchanged. The exact tests therefore run only in the environment of record, and everything else compares within stated tolerances. The image id changes with every commit because the history layer carries the commit. Git history enters the image through a git bundle that paper/container/build.sh passes in. The image pins Node.js for arm64, with its checksum and signature verified.

H.3 Commands¶

The tests assume a git checkout of the repository: a few read its history, and in a copy made without it they skip with a reason. The last row of the table regenerates one row of H.4, including the cells it needs.

Command	What it does	A pass looks like
uv sync --locked	install the locked environment	exits 0
uv lock --check	check that the lock matches pyproject.toml	exits 0
uv run pytest	run the test suite, doctests included	all tests pass
uv run python -m derivation.run	run the chain L1 to L22	every lemma reports PASS
uv run python -m derivation.proofmap	check the proof map against the scripts	exits 0
uv run python paper/execute_notebooks.py	execute both notebooks from a clean kernel and write the outputs	exits 0. The outputs are unchanged when the data and the seeds are: git diff --stat -- paper/paper.ipynb paper/supporting_material.ipynb then shows only the commit and platform lines of the toolchain table, and PAPER_EXECUTE=1 uv run pytest tests/test_paper.py -k clean_kernel compares every other text output of a fresh run with the committed one
uv run python paper/regenerate_row.py ROW	regenerate one row of the table in H.4 (ROW is its first column): runs the cells that row needs and prints the table or saves the figure, writing nothing in the repository	prints the table, equal to that cell's committed output, or names the figure file
cd paper && uv run myst build --html	build the paper as a static site, written to paper/_build/html	exits 0, with no error reported. It prints a line that a server started on a local port, because the builder renders the pages through a temporary server that stops when the build ends
